---
title: DEEP16 art in hand -- what came back, and the pastes kept for a re-roll
made: 2026-10-08 (Code tab), split out of deep16-art-wanted.md on Griz's word ("shouldn't we have (a better titled) art_we_used_to_want.md or something, such that deep16-art-wanted can just be copypasta to throw in the image gen as I can?" -- then "The split will be a thing")
how: nothing here is wanted. When a block on `deep16-art-wanted.md` comes back and is cut, or is ruled not wanted, it moves here -- a line under BUILT FROM THE LIST with the date and the commit, and its paste into its own section for a re-roll -- never deleted. Everything below the list is the old register's DONE half as it stood at the split, word for word. **A `deep16/_src/Fresh/` path below is where a sheet came in:** since 2026-10-14 every used sheet lives in `deep16/_src/<creature>/` (Fresh is the inbox again, Griz's word; the rule is the `how:` of `deep16-art-wanted.md`).
---

## The old COMMON TAIL (what the heads below were pasted with; the wanted file's blocks carry their own)

16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Flat dark navy background. Top left: one large portrait. Beside it a turnaround of four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left. Below: labelled rows of frames, every frame in side view facing right, numbered under each frame. BEHOLDA, SHEET 2 -- attach her first sheet (the one with the Big Screen Projection row):

Attached: Beholda's first sprite sheet. Draw her again exactly: the same character, the same pixel style, the same colours, the same size. A round fuzzy purple body with one big eye and a wide smile, four eyestalks with red-ringed eyes on top, cyan lightning wisps at her sides, and three dice hanging on short chains below her (a cyan d20 on the left, a magenta d10 in the middle, a lime d8 on the right).

HEAD: BEHOLDA, THE BENEVOLENT BEHOLDER -- sheet 2, her moves for a tactics game. She has no feet: she floats, and in every frame the bottom of her lowest die hangs the same small height above one shared baseline. She has no hands: she fights by swinging her dangling dice and with her big eye. Rows: Hover (8: drifting forward, the eyestalks trailing back, the dice swinging behind her); Dice Slam (6: she twists and swings her three dice on their chains at a foe in front of her like a flail, then they swing back); VNA Bubble (6: her eyestalks flare out wide, her big eye squeezes shut and then flies open, and a shimmering rainbow soap-bubble film forms close around her body, never bigger than her own body); Baleful Gaze (6: she turns her big eye full on a foe in front of her; the eye swells and glows violet with a hypnotic spiral in the pupil; no beam leaves the eye); Flinch (4: struck, she squashes and jolts back with the eyestalks splayed, then recovers); Fall (6: knocked out, she sinks to the ground, the eyestalks droop, the big eye rolls up and closes, the dice clatter down; the last frame lies flat on the ground); Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: lying on the ground, then pushing herself back up into the air).

Eerie, a little Lovecraftian, but friendly in how he moves.

HEAD: GOOSE THE LOBSTAMONKEE -- sheet 1, his moves for a tactics game. He is small and springy and never stands still. Rows: Idle (6: bouncing up and down on the spot on his bent legs, the feelers bobbing with each bounce, the tail curling and uncurling); Hop (8: travelling in little bouncing hops, the long arms swinging, the tail held out behind for balance); Sling (6: his regular blow: he whirls a sling over his head in one hand and lets fly forward, then follows through; the stone small); Cast (6: he crouches low, springs straight up into the air with both arms raised high over his head and the clawed hands spread wide, a soft glow between the hands at the top of the jump, then lands back in a crouch); Honk (4: he thrusts his head forward with his mouth wide open and the feelers flaring out, a loud honk); Climb (6: climbing straight up an unseen wall with the clawed hands, the feet and the tail; do not draw the wall); Flinch (4: struck, he jolts back with his eyes squeezed shut and the feelers whipping, then recovers); Fall (6: knocked out, he topples over and lies curled on his side, the feelers limp; the last frame lies on the ground); Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: lying on the ground, then bouncing back up).

16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Flat dark navy background. Top left: one large portrait. Beside it a turnaround of four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left. Below: labelled rows of frames, every frame in side view facing right, numbered under each frame. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. The same scale in every row, feet on one baseline. No scenery except what a row names.

**All five came back 10-07** (GPT, so `deep16/_src/goose_gpt_3..5.png` and `rascal_gpt_4..5.png`, not the grok names below) and are cut: Goose's S and N rows and his `grouphug` and `lifeline` in `goose_p1` (`tools/goose-sheet.py`, the specs in `tools/goose-fronts-spec.py`; GPT drew him lighter and warmer, each channel brought to his first sheets' charcoal), Rascal's S and N rows in `rascal_p2` (`tools/rascal-sheet-p2.py` sheets 4 and 5; his climb from behind its first five of six, to match the side climb's five).

Griz, 10-07: *"can you give me the copy pasta for the animation sheets that would round out goose and rascal better - they need norths and souths and ...?"* Every sheet so far drew them side-on only, so facing S and N on the grid they play their side rows (Denny's and Beholda's the same, ruled fine for them: *"fine enough, plenty else to do"*). These four ask for each move that is aimed at someone or walks somewhere, from the front (facing S) and from behind (facing N). **The seat's calls, his to overrule:** the bow, the dance and the spin (Social Sharing, Social Flame, Social Distancing) are left side-on -- they read from any side; Fall and Prone are left side-on -- a body on the floor reads from any side; Climb is asked from behind only (a wall is climbed facing it). No portrait and no turnaround on these: rows only, so the rows get the room. When they come back: `deep16/_src/goose_grok_4.png` (front) and `goose_grok_5.png` (behind), `rascal_grok_5.png` (front) and `rascal_grok_6.png` (behind); the cutters (`tools/goose-sheet.py`, `tools/rascal-sheet-p2.py`) take the front rows for facing S and the behind rows for facing N, the diagonals keeping the side rows; the scale by the same measure as now (Goose's torso, Rascal's hat brim).

### The goblin, redone (added 10-07, on Griz's word)

Griz, 10-07: *"Redoing goblins while we have an expanded art department, please the copypasta for all the 16 bit sprite animation rows we'll need for them"*. The goblin is the Game Show's first wave (its lane §3 D: *"the most famous monsters that fit the CR requirements (i.e. goblins)"*) and a Quaternius Blob Orc till now (`goblin_p1`: idle, walk, attack). Its kit (`deep16/data/foes.js` goblin, SRD 5.1): Scimitar, Shortbow (loosed when nothing is in reach), Nimble Escape as Disengage and a step back (`deep16/js/traits.js`); the SRD's other half of Nimble Escape, Hide as a bonus action, is not built for it. The look follows the 8-bit's goblin (`js/bestiary-art.js` A.goblin: green skin, yellow eyes, the ears swept wide, brown leather, a scimitar), with the SRD's AC 15 drawn as leather and a shield.

**Came back 10-07, IN HAND** (Griz: *"Should be GPT Goblins in _src"*; `deep16/_src/Goblin-GPT1..4.png`, all four, every row asked for): `goblin_p2` by `tools/goblin-sheet.py` over `tools/sheetrows.py` -- each sheet scaled by the figure's area against sheet 1's turnaround (about 36 hunched from the side, 41 from the front), sheets 2-4 toned to sheet 1's olive (GPT drew them a yellower green), the back idle's five frames played 1 2 3 4 5 4; Nimble Escape's hop plays before its steps (`deep16/js/traits.js`), the arrow leaves on the shortbow's release frame (the row's `release`, `deep16/js/battle.js`), Hide is cut but nothing plays it; the wagon's glamour goblins wear it too. `goblin_p1` stays on disk till your look. The pastes below are kept for a re-roll.

**Four sheets. Sheet 1 first; sheets 2-4 attach what sheet 1 brought back.** Sheet 1 is the side rows with the portrait and turnaround; sheet 2 its tricks, side-on; sheet 3 from the front (facing S), sheet 4 from behind (facing N). The seat's calls, the Goose/Rascal precedent: Fall and Prone side-on only (a body on the floor reads from any side), Hide side-on only, Climb side and behind (a wall is climbed facing it). Every row has one frame count in every facing (Idle 6, Walk 8, Scimitar 6, Shortbow 6, Nimble Escape 4, Flinch 4, Climb 6). No arrow in flight anywhere (the grid draws its own, `FX.projectile`; a loose arrow would be cut onto the nearest frame).

**When they come back:** `deep16/_src/goblin_<gpt|grok>_1..4.png`, a cutter `tools/goblin-sheet.py` over `tools/sheetrows.py` to `goblin_p2` (then `foes.js` goblin `sheet`). Rows: turnaround -> idle by facing where a facing has no row; Idle -> idle; Walk -> walk; Scimitar -> `scimitar` (battle.js plays a row named for the attack) and `attack` (the fallback); Shortbow -> `shortbow`; Nimble Escape -> `nimble` (new: `tools/pixelate.py` ANIM_ORDER and FPS, `deep16/js/ui.js`'s play-once rule, the "darts back" in `deep16/js/traits.js` plays it before the walk); Hide -> `hide` (new; nothing plays it until the goblin's Hide is built); Climb -> climb; Flinch -> flinch; Fall -> hurt; Prone -> prone (`S.proneRow`). Sheet 3's rows for facing S, sheet 4's for N, the diagonals the side rows. Then `deep16/?show=goblin` and a fresh eyes row.

GOBLIN, SHEET 1 (the look, side-on; no reference to attach):

HEAD: THE GOBLIN -- sheet 1, a foe for a tactics game. A goblin: a small, scrawny, wiry humanoid, hunched, about half a man's height, with green skin, a flat face with a broad flat nose, a wide mouth of small sharp teeth, small yellow eyes, and long pointed ears sweeping out and up from the sides of its head. A scruffy brown leather jerkin with a dark belt, dark brown leggings, bare clawed feet. A curved steel scimitar in its right hand, a small round wooden shield strapped to its left forearm, a shortbow and a quiver of arrows slung across its back. Sly and quick, never quite still. Rows: Idle (6: crouched on bent knees, shifting its weight, its head darting left and right and its ears twitching, the scimitar held low and ready); Walk (8: a quick hunched scurry, the scimitar low, the shield up); Scimitar (6: a quick vicious slash at a foe in front of it, then back on guard); Shortbow (6: the scimitar hung at its belt, it pulls an arrow from the quiver on its back, nocks it, draws the shortbow with the bow in its shield hand and looses; draw no arrow in flight -- the last frame holds the empty bow); Flinch (4: struck, it jolts back with its eyes squeezed shut and its ears flattened, then recovers); Fall (6: knocked out, it topples backward and lies flat on its back, the scimitar dropped beside it; the last frame lies on the ground); Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: lying on the ground, then pushing itself up).

### The bugbear (added 10-07, on Griz's word: one file, every sheet)

Griz, 10-07: *"try me the bugbear as an .md with all the sheets listed and we'll see what comes out"*. The four sheets are one file for the generator, **`deep16-art-bugbear.md`** at this folder's root: the look and the style once, then sheets 1-4 (side with the portrait and turnaround; its tricks side-on; from the front; from behind), the goblin's shape. Nothing in it is for a seat; the row map is here. Its kit (`deep16/data/foes.js` bugbear, SRD 5.1): Morningstar (Brute is in its 2d8), the Javelin thrown when nothing is in reach, Surprise Attack (+2d6 on a hit in round one, `deep16/js/battle.js` att.surprise). The look follows the 8-bit's (`js/bestiary-art.js` A.bugbear: tan-brown hide, brown fur and ruff, pointed ears, big) with the SRD's AC 16 drawn as hide armour and a shield.

**Came back 10-07, IN HAND** -- GPT drew the file's four sheets as four panels of ONE 1536 x 1024 image, twice (Griz: *"the bugbear came back from gpt as ogre - but they are in _src for review"*; `deep16/_src/Ogre Warrior GPT.png`, `Ogre_GPT.png`), and he ruled the two *"Bugbear and chief"*: the shaggy one is `bugbear_p1`, the tusked one with the topknot `bugbearchief_p2` (the chief had shared the bugbear's Monkroose, `bugbearchief_p1`, kept on disk till his look). Both cut by `tools/bugbear-sheet.py` over `tools/sheetrows.py`. **What the one-file test found: a file with every sheet comes back as one image, so every frame is about game size** (the side idle 46 px on the image) -- nothing to downscale, softer than the goblin's (its sheets 2-4x game size). For sharp frames: one sheet per image. The seat's calls: the front idle about 56 px (the chief 60, the boss), the rest by area against each image's turnaround (the bugbear's side rows a 7% upscale); both images' from-behind Flinch is drawn facing the viewer, so N plays the side flinch; the chief's side Flinch is drawn facing the viewer too and stands in (1 2 3 1); the chief's Fall is drawn 1 2 3 5 6 2, cut as down on 1 2 3 5 with 6 and 5 its prone; the bugbear's Fall lies flat from its first frame; nine-frame walks lose their doubled number; the chief's javelin rows are not cut (no javelin in its `foes.js` block) nor its seventh, unnumbered climber. Lurk is `still`; Ambush plays for a round-one melee blow from a foe with `surprise` (`deep16/js/battle.js`); the javelin leaves on frame 4 (`release`).

### The hobgoblin and the hobgoblin sergeant (added 10-07, on Griz's word)

Griz, 10-07: *"copy pasta for 3 and 4 please"* (3: the next goblinoids, the bestiary lane §6.4; 4: the Game Show's art asks, below). Both are Game Show wave foes and still Quaternius stand-ins (`hobgoblin_p1` the Orc, `hobsergeant_p1` the Orc Skull). Their kits (`deep16/data/foes.js`): the **hobgoblin** (SRD 5.1, CR 1/2, AC 18: chain mail and a shield) has a Longsword, a Longbow it looses when nothing is in reach, and Martial Advantage (+2d6 once a turn while an ally stands by the target, `deep16/js/battle.js`). The **sergeant** (game-original from the SRD's Hobgoblin and Veteran, CR 3, AC 18) swings the Longsword twice a turn and has Martial Advantage, but no bow. The look follows the 8-bit's (`js/bestiary-art.js` A.hobgoblin: orange skin, dark hair, a dark red tunic, a grey helm, a sword, a brown shield, a gold belt; the sergeant is the same figure tinted darker red).

**Four sheets each, one sheet an image** (the bugbear's finding: a file of every sheet came back as one packed image with soft frames). Sheet 1 first; sheets 2-4 attach what sheet 1 brought back. Same calls as the goblin: Fall and Prone side-on only, Climb side and behind, one frame count for each row in every facing, no arrow in flight.

**IN HAND (10-07, his: *"Hobgoblin and hobsergeant sheets are in _src"*):** `deep16/_src/Hobgoblin1..4.png` and `HobSergeant1..4.png`, cut by `tools/hobgoblin-sheet.py` to `hobgoblin_p2` and `hobsergeant_p2`, worn by both foes; `deep16/?show=hobgoblin,hobsergeant`. **As asked:** `deep16/_src/`, cut by a `tools/hobgoblin-sheet.py` over `tools/sheetrows.py` to `hobgoblin_p2` and `hobsergeant_p2` (then `foes.js` `sheet`). Rows: Idle -> idle; Walk -> walk; Longsword -> `longsword` and `attack`; **Longsword 2 -> `longsword2`** (battle.js plays an attack's second use in a turn on its numbered row, as claw2 does; it goes in pixelate's ANIM_ORDER); Longbow -> `longbow` (with a `release` frame, as the goblin's shortbow has); **Martial Advantage -> `martial`** (new: the drilled lunge, played on a melee blow when an ally stands by the target and the turn's Martial Advantage is unspent; on a miss too, since the drill is visible either way; the seat's call); Flinch -> flinch; Fall -> hurt; Prone -> prone; Climb -> climb. Sheet 3 plays facing S, sheet 4 facing N, the diagonals use the side rows. Then `deep16/?show=hobgoblin` and `?show=hobsergeant` and a fresh eyes row.

HOBGOBLIN, SHEET 1 (the look, side-on; nothing to attach):

HEAD: THE HOBGOBLIN -- sheet 1, a foe for a tactics game. A hobgoblin: a goblinoid soldier, as tall as a man, upright and disciplined, broad in the shoulder. Dark orange skin, a flat face with a broad flat nose, a heavy brow over small yellow eyes, pointed ears swept back, long black hair tied back. A plain steel helm with a nose guard; a coat of chain mail over a dark red tunic; a belt with a gold buckle; dark trousers and boots. A longsword in its right hand; a rectangular wooden shield banded in iron on its left arm; a longbow and a quiver of arrows slung across its back. A drilled soldier: it holds the line and never wastes a move. Rows: Idle (6: standing on guard behind its shield, the longsword held level, breathing slowly, its eyes moving across the field); Walk (8: a steady marching step, the shield up and the sword held ready); Longsword (6: a disciplined cut at a foe in front of it, stepping in behind the shield, then back on guard); Longbow (6: the longsword sheathed at its hip, it pulls an arrow from the quiver on its back, nocks it, draws the longbow with the bow in its shield hand and looses; draw no arrow in flight -- the last frame holds the empty bow); Flinch (4: struck, it rocks back behind its shield with its teeth gritted, then steadies); Fall (6: knocked out from STANDING: frame 1 on its feet and reeling, frame 2 tipping backward, frame 3 falling, frames 4 to 6 hitting the ground and lying flat on its back, the longsword dropped beside it); Prone (2: knocked flat but alive: lying on its side on the ground, then pushing itself up on one arm).

### The Game Show's props: the chest and the bed (added 10-07, on Griz's word)

The Game Show lane §3 F. **The chest** is where the audience's supplies land (seat 1's words for Ingrith: *"The chest by the lamp -- whatever the folk up top send down for you lands in it."*; the epic amulet's pop-up says "check the chest!"). Nothing draws it today. **The bed** is where the rests are taken between waves. Third Lamp's cots are the 8-bit's `y` tiles, which the grid draws as `deep16/js/iso.js` BLOCK `y`, a timber crib with slats and a bar, so they read as cradles, not beds. Both are props on one floor diamond each, drawn from a sheet as `deep16/js/circles.js` draws the wheel. The bed's glow when its rest is due is the code's job, not the art's.

**IN HAND (10-07):** his two sheets (`deep16/_src/Pixel Art Treasure Chest Animation Sheet.png`, `Pixel Art Cot Comparison A and B.png`) cut by `tools/gsprops-sheet.py` to `gschest_p1` (rows `shut`, `thump`, `open`, `opened`) and `gsbed_p1` (`a`, `b`: the two diagonals); the chest stands at Third Lamp's tower (`lampcircle` `chest`), the cots are drawn from the sheet instead of the crib (`cots`), `deep16/js/circles.js`. Seen in the pane: *"looks great from here in the pane"* · *"cots too"*.

PROPS, THE SUPPLY CHEST (one image):

HEAD: PROPS, THE SUPPLY CHEST -- a dwarven supply chest at a lamp station deep underground, where gifts from the surface arrive by magic. One sturdy chest, wider than it is deep: dark oak planks bound with iron bands, riveted iron corners, a heavy domed lid, a big iron lock plate on its front, and a small brass lamp emblem set in the middle of the lid. Its front faces the lower left. Rows: Shut (1: closed and still); Thump (3: something lands inside it from above: the chest jolts, the lid jumps open a crack with a flash of gold light, then slams shut with a puff of dust); Open (4: the lid swings up from shut to wide open, warm gold light spilling out from inside); Opened (2: the lid wide open, the inside glowing warm gold with a few small sparkles, the two frames flickering).

16-bit pixel art props, SNES-era, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric view from above at three-quarters, the floor tiles diamonds twice as wide as they are tall; light from the upper left. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet only, filling the whole image: no other panels, no title. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped. Every frame the same chest at the same scale and in the same spot in its frame, standing on one floor diamond that it mostly fills, with clear space around it. No characters, no floor drawn, no labels on the prop.

PROPS, THE CAMP COT (one image):

HEAD: PROPS, THE CAMP COT -- a dwarves' camp cot at a lamp station deep underground, where tired heroes rest between fights. A low, sturdy wooden frame on four short legs, a taut canvas sling between the rails, a grey wool blanket folded at the foot, a small straw pillow at the head. Draw it twice, each alone: A (1: lying along the floor's diagonal from the upper left to the lower right, the pillow at the upper left); B (1: the same cot lying along the other diagonal, from the upper right to the lower left, the pillow at the upper right).

16-bit pixel art props, SNES-era, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric view from above at three-quarters, the floor tiles diamonds twice as wide as they are tall; light from the upper left. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet only, filling the whole image: no other panels, no title. Each cot labelled A or B beside it, the same scale for both, each lying on one floor diamond and filling it lengthwise from corner to corner, with clear space around it. No characters, no floor drawn, no labels on the prop.

### In the 8-bit with no grid foe: all seven now on the grid (10-05)

The Hired Blade, the Stable Fighter and the Drow Blade-Captain are done above. The four creatures are **drawn in code** (Griz: *"can we give them a special effect body?"* -- *"Let's try code drawn"*), so they are in hand, and a real sheet or model is welcome, never needed:

- the **will-o'-wisp**: a light field (`tools/wisp-sheet.py`), with a floor light of its own (`glow`, out when it turns invisible);
- the **stirge**, the **fire beetle** (glands that light the floor) and the **giant centipede**: `tools/bugs-sheet.py` on `tools/codeart.py`; the stirge has a `latched` row for Blood Drain (the proboscis driven in, the body swelling red).

Front and back views for the three bugs are in (S and N, 10-05), and so is the hurt row from the front and behind for all three, his GPT sheets (10-08: the stirge and the fire beetle `7a85cf3`, the centipede `3a3f0e1`). What is left: a wisp that shows its Variable Illumination (bright 5-20 ft) on screen.

Any of them goes first to a free printable with a .blend (`deep16/blender-monsters.md`), second to a generated sheet. A second sheet for something already in hand (idle variations, special moves) is welcome, never needed.

### Heads kept for a re-roll (all IN HAND above)

HEAD: GRICK -- the dwarven road. A thick worm-like body with rubbery grey-green hide, the head a sharp beak ringed by four tentacles. It rears up from a coil to strike. Rows: Idle (coiled, 8), Slither (8), Attack (rears, tentacles then beak, 8), Hurt (6), Death (8).

HEAD: THE KEEPER (a serpent of living water) -- the flooded dwarven stair. A serpent made of clear blue-green water with a faint face in it, rising out of a pool on a worn stone stair. Every frame rises out of the same pool surface. Rows: Hidden (only ripples on the pool, 6), Rise (8), Idle (8), Strike (8), Constrict (coils round a shape and drags it under, 8), Hurt (a burst of spray, 6), Death (falls back into the pool, 8).

HEAD: DUERGAR (grey dwarves) -- the deep. Bald grey-skinned dwarves with pale eyes, dark iron scale armour, a war pick and javelins. Rows: Idle (8), Walk (8), Attack (war pick, 8), Throw (javelin, 6), Enlarge (grows to twice his height, 6), Fade (turns invisible, 6), Hurt (6), Death (8).

HEAD: TROLL -- the dwarven road. Tall and lanky, rubbery green hide, long arms dragging clawed hands, a long nose, lank dark hair. Large. Rows: Idle (8), Walk (8), Claws (8), Bite (6), Hurt (6), Death (burning, 8).

HEAD: STONE GIANT -- the deep. Lean and hairless, grey skin like carved stone, heavy brow. Huge: much taller than a man. Rows: Idle (8), Walk (8), Club (8), Throw Rock (8), Catch Rock (6), Hurt (6), Death (8).

### The landlord's pictures (the telepathy in the Wet -- stills, not sheets; asked 09-30g; IN HAND 10-01)

**In:** his five from `dev/visions/` (10-01), cut by `tools/visions.py` to 360 wide and a palette each, drawn by `deep16/js/wet.js` `W.picture` (the code's 96 x 72 sketches stand in only till they load). The prompts below are kept for a re-roll.

The landlord's own sheet is in (IN HAND above); these are different. The otyugh speaks in pictures. On the grid each one fills the screen for a few seconds, swaying, breathing, a vignette closing in (`deep16/js/wet.js` `W.picture`). The order is the order they are seen: the
bucket at first contact, then the other four in a row after it is fed ("what comes down the stream").

#### COMMON TAIL (pictures -- paste after each picture's head)

A single still illustration, not a sprite sheet. Painterly dark fantasy, murky and dreamlike, as if seen through dirty water and through someone else's mind: soft edges, a dark vignette closing in from every corner, a sickly green-grey palette with only the small accents the head names. 4:3 landscape. No text, no border, no frame, no people.

#### P1. The bucket (first contact: it is hungry)

HEAD: A PICTURE OF A BUCKET -- the landlord's first thought. A wooden bucket bound with iron hoops, lowered on a frayed rope out of the dark above, swinging a little; bones and scraps of carrion over its rim; thick drips falling from its bottom into black water far below. Behind the whole scene, huge and blurred, a single yellow eye.

#### P2. The fall

HEAD: THE FALL -- an underground waterfall pouring out of a crack high in a cave wall into a black pool; the stream carries bones, broken timbers and rags down with it; spray and mist; cold blue-grey water light.

#### P3. The crook (the clacker cavern upstream)

HEAD: THE CROOK -- a cavern forest of giant pale fungus, tall caps in lilac and bone-white glowing faintly over a still, mirror-dark cave lake; in the foreground the stream leaves the lake through a narrow channel.

#### P4. The clackers (hook horrors; the game never names them)

HEAD: THE CLACKERS -- two tall hunched hook horrors in near darkness: vulture-like beaked heads, armoured grey bodies, long arms ending in huge curved bone hooks raised mid-clack; only their silhouettes, the pale hooks and small yellow eyes catch the light.

#### P5. The chimney of wings

HEAD: THE CHIMNEY -- a narrow natural rock chimney seen from below, rising into darkness, filled with a spiral of thousands of bats streaming upward; a thin shaft of grey light far above.

### The clackers (the landlord's picture made flesh; asked 10-01; the sheet IN HAND 10-01)

Griz, 10-01: *"we're going to need an art-prompt description of the clackers it has envisioned to hope for something close when we eventually model it, try make one of those in blender so we have it later"*, and the same night, *"I had a problem with the 'later' on the clacker and there's a 16 bit animation sheet available for review"*: he ran Q2, and the sheet is on the grid (IN HAND above; `?npc=clacker` fields it, a block of our own in `deep16/data/foes.js` and `invented.json#clacker`, clacking its hooks at the start of each turn). The look below is read off the landlord's picture (`dev/visions/the-clackers.jpg`, the fourth it sends when fed) and the wiki's clacker cavern (`TarlynsPit/wiki/the-warrens.md`: a hook horror colony crowning the crook; the clacking is their language). The game never names them. Q1 (the model sheet) is still welcome for the Blender model below.

**The look, in words (for any prompt, any tool):** a hook horror. Tall and hunched, about nine feet if it ever straightened, its back bowed so the head hangs forward below the line of the shoulders. A small vulture's head on a thick neck: a long down-curved beak, pale bone, hooked at the tip; two small round yellow eyes set close above the beak's root; no ears, no crest. The whole body sheathed in small overlapping plates like cobblestones or old scales, dark green-grey, lighter on the ridges and black in the seams; a heavy chest and shoulders, a narrow waist. No hands: each long forearm ends in one huge curved hook of pale ivory bone, longer than the forearm, smooth and worn bright on its inner edge. At rest the hooks are carried raised and folded back, so they rise above the shoulders and curl forward over the head like a pair of scythes. Long bent legs, clawed feet, a stalking gait. The only colours are the grey-green hide, the bone of the beak and hooks, and the yellow of the eyes. It talks by striking its hooks together: clack, clack.

#### Q1. The clacker, a model sheet (for modelling it in 3D)

Paste alone (not with the COMMON TAIL above):

HEAD: CHARACTER MODEL SHEET FOR 3D MODELLING -- a hook horror: [paste the look, in words, above]. Orthographic views of the whole creature side by side, the same scale and the same baseline in each: FRONT, SIDE (facing right), BACK, and THREE-QUARTER FRONT; in each view it stands in its neutral hunch with the hooks raised over the shoulders. Beneath: a close-up of the head in profile and from the front (the beak, the eyes, how the plates run over the skull), a close-up of one forearm and its hook from the side (where bone meets plate), and a small figure of a human man beside the side view for scale. Flat even studio lighting, no cast shadows, no perspective, a plain light-grey background, clean lines. Concept art, not a painting: every form readable. No text but the view labels.

#### Q2. The clacker, a sprite sheet (for the grid, the day it fights)

HEAD: THE CLACKER -- a hook horror out of the deep caves: [paste the look, in words, above]. Large: twice a man's height hunched. Rows: Idle (8, the head bobbing, the hooks flexing), Walk (8, the hunched stride, hooks held high), Hook (8, one hook swung down and across), Clack (6, both hooks raised and struck together over the head: their speech and their war-cry), Climb (6, hooking up a rock wall), Hurt (6), Death (8).

**The model (10-01, a first pass):** `tools/clacker-blend.py` builds it from nothing in Blender 5.2 (`"C:/Program Files/Blender Foundation/Blender 5.2/blender.exe" -b --python tools/clacker-blend.py [-- previews]` writes `deep16/_src/clacker/clacker.blend`, gitignored, so the script is what the repo keeps). A Skin-modifier body with a plated-hide shader, swept bone hooks, beak and toe claws, yellow eyes; 23 bones; IDLE, WALK and ATTACK (the hooks raised in a V, crossed in an X at frame 17: the clack). It is in `tools/deep16-figures.json` as `clacker` (1.35 squares, about 86 px tall, the ettin's height) and runs through `tools/render-sprites.py`; nothing in the game uses it yet. Previews: `dev/visions/clacker-model-*.png`. **Short of the painting:** the body is a round blob, the legs frog-like, the head long rather than a domed vulture's skull, the hooks rod-straight from the front; a Q1 model sheet is what would true it.

Spell looks are NOT on this list: the pass draws them in code (and Blender can render a floating weapon), so the generator's days go to creatures.

### The water elemental (asked 10-08 in §1; drawn 10-10 by the art desk, ART-08; CUT 10-14, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

The block as it stood on `deep16-art-wanted.md` §1, word for word, kept for a re-roll.

#### The water elemental (not a foe yet)

RULED 10-04 (Griz: *"we're staying as close to the SRD as we can, immunity and a sheet"*): a foe of its own with the SRD's condition immunities and its own sheet. Nothing plays this until the foe is built.

```text
THE WATER ELEMENTAL -- a foe for a tactics game, drawn on two sheets. A churning column of living water twice a man's height: a rough head and two heavy arms of surging water rising out of a rolling wave at its base, clear blue-green, white foam at its edges, bubbles drifting inside it. It has no legs: it rolls on its wave.

HEAD 1 -- THE WATER ELEMENTAL from the bottom-right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the bottom-right corner of the image. Rows: Idle (6: surging and churning in place, foam curling at its edges); Move (8: it rolls toward the bottom-right on its wave, leaning into the motion); Slam (6: a heavy arm of water rises and crashes down on a foe toward the bottom-right, spraying, then reforms); Whelm (8: it rears up tall and crashes toward the bottom-right over a space as big as a man, closing round it, then holds, swollen, as if something is inside it -- do not draw what it holds); Flinch (4: struck, it splashes apart at the blow and pulls back together); Fall (6: it loses its shape and collapses into a spreading pool; the last frame a flat puddle on the ground).

HEAD 2 -- THE WATER ELEMENTAL from the top-right, seen from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the top-right corner of the image, rows only. Rows: Idle (6: surging and churning in place, foam curling at its edges); Move (8: it rolls toward the top-right on its wave, leaning into the motion); Slam (6: a heavy arm of water rises and crashes down toward the top-right, spraying, then reforms); Whelm (8: it rears up tall and crashes toward the top-right over a space as big as a man, closing round it, then holds, swollen -- do not draw what it holds); Flinch (4: struck from behind, it splashes apart at the blow and pulls back together, still facing away); Fall (6: it loses its shape and collapses into a spreading pool; the last frame a flat puddle on the ground).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title. Only the first sheet has a portrait and a turnaround -- top left one large portrait, beside it four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left; every later sheet is rows only and draws the creature exactly as the first sheet's turnaround shows it, the same look, colours and size. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame faces the way its HEAD says; a walk is animated in place, not travelling across the sheet. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. The same scale in every row and on every sheet, the bottom of every frame on one baseline. No scenery except what a row names.
```
Cut as: Move -> walk, Slam -> `slam` (and `slam2`), Whelm -> `whelm` (new, wired with its foe), Fall -> hurt; HEAD 1 plays SE, S and E (SW and W mirrored), HEAD 2 NE and N (NW mirrored); a row on HEAD 1 only plays from it in every facing. No prone (SRD: immune). Large.

#### The black pudding (today: the green Slime)

```text
THE BLACK PUDDING -- a foe for a tactics game, drawn on two sheets. A heaving heap of glossy black slime that flows like hot tar, never solid: it sags, spreads and runs, a dull purple sheen where the light catches its curves, drips running down it and slow bubbles breaking on its skin. No face, no eyes, no limbs.

HEAD 1 -- THE BLACK PUDDING from the bottom-right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the bottom-right corner of the image. Rows: Idle (6: it heaves and settles, a bubble swelling and bursting); Ooze (8: it rolls toward the bottom-right along the ground, its front edge folding over itself); Pseudopod (6: a thick black arm of slime whips out toward the bottom-right and smashes down on a foe, the floor smoking where it lands, then draws back in); Flinch (4: struck, it splashes and caves in where the blow landed, then swells back); Fall (6: it collapses and spreads flat into a black puddle; the last frame a flat smear on the ground).

HEAD 2 -- THE BLACK PUDDING from the top-right, seen from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the top-right corner of the image, rows only. Rows: Climb (6: seen from behind, it flows straight up an unseen wall in front of it, flattened against it; do not draw the wall).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title. Only the first sheet has a portrait and a turnaround -- top left one large portrait, beside it four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left; every later sheet is rows only and draws the creature exactly as the first sheet's turnaround shows it, the same look, colours and size. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame faces the way its HEAD or its row says; a walk is animated in place, not travelling across the sheet. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. The same scale in every row and on every sheet, the bottom of every frame on one baseline. No scenery except what a row names.
```
Cut as: Ooze -> walk, Pseudopod -> `pseudopod`, Climb -> climb, Fall -> hurt; HEAD 1 plays every facing (the left ones mirrored); its Climb from HEAD 2. Split's halves `puddingm_p1`. No prone (immune). Large.

#### The gelatinous cube (today: the green Slime) -- BACK: `deep16/_src/Fresh/Gelatinous Cube Animation Sheet-3.png`

```text
HEAD: THE GELATINOUS CUBE -- a foe for a tactics game. A ten-foot cube of clear, faintly teal jelly, almost invisible, its edges and corners catching the light; inside it hang the things it has swallowed and not yet dissolved: a few bones, a rusted sword, a scatter of coins, an old boot. No face, no eyes, no limbs. Rows: Idle (6: it quivers in place, the things inside drifting slowly); Slide (8: it slides forward along the ground, its whole body wobbling, the things inside lagging behind); Engulf (8: its front face bulges out and surges forward over a space as big as a man, then closes and draws back in, swollen -- do not draw what it takes); Digest (6: the jelly churns and bubbles inside it, the things it holds spinning slowly); Flinch (4: struck, it wobbles hard and dents where the blow landed, then firms again); Fall (6: it loses its corners, sags and spreads into a wide shining puddle, the things inside spilling out onto the floor; the last frame lies flat on the ground).

16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet only, filling the whole image: no other panels. Top left: one large portrait. Beside it a turnaround of four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left. Below: labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, every frame in side view facing right, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. The same scale in every row, the bottom of every frame on one baseline. No scenery except what a row names.
```

### Prone rows for looks in hand, as one paste (pulled 10-08 to the chat)

Griz, 10-08: *"If there's anything else in the art-wanted sheet, please pull it out and show me here."* The nine one-row asks of the wanted file's §3 made one paste in his new shape (the attached paragraph, a HEAD each, one tail) and handed to him in chat; if they do not come back, the paste goes back on the wanted file. The paste:

ATTACH, in this order: `deep16/_src/chuul_grok_1.webp`, `crawler_grok_1.webp`, `ettercap_grok_1.png`, `otyugh_grok_1.webp`, `bulette_grok_1.jpg`, `owl_grok_1.jpg`, `snowyowl_grok_1.jpg`, `boar_grok_1.webp`, `clacker_grok_2.jpg`
```text
Attached: nine sprite sheets from the same game, in this order -- 1 the chuul, 2 the crawler, 3 the ettercap, 4 the otyugh, 5 the bulette, 6 the brown owl, 7 the snowy owl, 8 the giant boar, 9 the clacker. Each HEAD below asks one more row for one of them: draw that creature again exactly -- the same creature, the same pixel style, the same colours, the same size as its frames in its attached sheet -- in side view facing right.

HEAD 1 -- THE CHUUL (attached sheet 1), a hulking lobster-like creature with two great pincers and a cluster of tentacles round its mouth. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: flipped onto its back with its legs and pincers waving, then rolling back onto its legs).

HEAD 2 -- THE CRAWLER (attached sheet 2), a long many-legged crawling worm with a cluster of long tentacles round its mouth. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: flipped onto its back with its many legs waving, then twisting back over onto its legs).

HEAD 3 -- THE ETTERCAP (attached sheet 3), a hunched spider-like humanoid with long clawed arms and a fanged mouth. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: lying on its back, then rolling over and pushing itself up on its claws).

HEAD 4 -- THE OTYUGH (attached sheet 4), a bloated round body on three thick legs, a huge toothy mouth, two long grasping tentacles with spiked ends, and a third stalk bearing its eyes. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: tipped over on its side with its tentacles flailing, then righting itself on its three legs).

HEAD 5 -- THE BULETTE (attached sheet 5), a massive armoured burrowing beast with a crested back, a huge shark-like mouth and short powerful clawed legs. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: rolled onto its side with its legs kicking, then heaving itself back onto its feet).

HEAD 6 -- THE OWL (attached sheet 6), a brown owl. Rows: Prone (2: knocked out of the air but alive: sprawled on the ground with its wings spread flat, then flapping back up off the ground).

HEAD 7 -- THE SNOWY OWL (attached sheet 7), a white snowy owl flecked with dark spots. Rows: Prone (2: knocked out of the air but alive: sprawled on the ground with its wings spread flat, then flapping back up off the ground).

HEAD 8 -- THE GIANT BOAR (attached sheet 8), a huge bristling wild boar with long curved tusks. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: on its side with its legs kicking, then scrambling back onto its feet).

HEAD 9 -- THE CLACKER (attached sheet 9), a tall hunched hook horror with a vulture's beaked head, a plated grey-green hide and a huge curved bone hook for each forearm. Rows: Prone (2: knocked flat but alive and struggling: fallen on its side with its hooks scraping the ground, then pushing itself up on one hook).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one creature an image, never two in one image. Every image: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE row only: no portrait, no turnaround, no title, no other panels. The row's name at its left, its frames numbered under each one -- every number used once, one drawing for each number -- every frame apart from its neighbour. Drawn at exactly the size of its attached sheet's frames, not larger. No scenery.
```

### Not for the generator (the code's own)

### How a sheet should come back

**Special moves before detailed attacks (10-01d).** Griz: *"It's important for us to do the special move ones - like burrow and Earth Glide more than detailed attacks"*. A row for a special move (going under, coming up, a hide's reveal) outranks a second attack row; the grid has the hooks (js/ai.js burrower, still and reveal).

What the cutter needs from a sheet (the gnoll sheets were right): a turnaround of stills (front, right, back, left), then rows of frames all facing right in side view; every frame apart from its neighbours; one scale for the whole sheet. One sheet an image: a file of several sheets came back as one packed image with soft frames (the bugbear, 10-07).

**To add an ask:** copy the nearest block above and change the head; keep its tail whole. A creature's later sheets (from the front, from behind, its tricks) follow the goblin's and the hobgoblin's in `deep16-art-in-hand.md`.

```text
THE GRAY KEEPING'S MATRIARCH-CLERIC -- a foe for a tactics game, drawn on five sheets. A female gnoll priestess of the hyena-god: bigger and heavier than a common gnoll, broad-shouldered and long-armed, a spotted hyena's head with a greying muzzle and a heavy grey ruff, scarred ears, pale knowing eyes. Over hide and mail she wears a long mantle of grey-brown hyena pelts and a collar of small bones and teeth; skull fetishes hang at her belt. She carries a tall staff of dark wood crowned with a hyena skull, and when she prays its eye-sockets burn with a pale green light. Old and powerful, not frail.

HEAD 1 -- THE MATRIARCH-CLERIC from the bottom-right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the bottom-right corner of the image. Rows: Idle (6: standing tall, leaning on the skull-staff, the mantle stirring, head turning slowly); Walk (8: a heavy, deliberate stride toward the bottom-right, the staff swinging with her steps); Strike (6: she swings the skull-staff two-handed at a foe toward the bottom-right, then recovers); Pray (6: she raises the staff high in both hands, the skull's sockets flare pale green, a ring of green light pulses out at her feet, then she lowers it); Laugh (6: she throws back her head in a hyena's cackling laugh, shoulders shaking); Flinch (4: struck, she snarls and recoils, then steadies); Fall (6: she staggers, drops to her knees, and falls onto her side, the staff beside her; the last frame lies on the ground); Prone (2: knocked down but alive, pushing herself up on the staff).

HEAD 2 -- THE MATRIARCH-CLERIC from the top-right, seen from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the top-right corner of the image, rows only. The same rows as HEAD 1, each drawn from behind facing the top-right: Idle (6); Walk (8); Strike (6); Pray (6); Laugh (6); Flinch (4); Fall (6); Prone (2).

HEAD 3 -- THE MATRIARCH-CLERIC straight from the front, facing the viewer: rows only, the same rows and counts as HEAD 1.

HEAD 4 -- THE MATRIARCH-CLERIC from the right side in profile, facing right: rows only, the same rows and counts as HEAD 1.

HEAD 5 -- THE MATRIARCH-CLERIC straight from behind, facing away from the viewer: rows only, the same rows and counts as HEAD 1.

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title. Only the first sheet has a portrait and a turnaround -- top left one large portrait, beside it four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left; every later sheet is rows only and draws her exactly as the first sheet's turnaround shows her, the same look, colours and size. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame faces the way its HEAD says; a walk is animated in place, not travelling across the sheet. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. The same scale in every row and on every sheet, feet on one baseline. No scenery except what a row names.
```

#### 15. The Snoot's horse units and its wagon -- ASKED 2026-10-17 as ART-69, ART-70, ART-71 (the package `deep16/_src/art-desk/sent/ART-SNOOTHORSE-2026-10-17/`, and its .zip, for his hand to the art desk)

Three new figures, each in all five views from its own first sheet, horse and rider (or wagon, team and wagoneer) one drawing a frame:
- **ART-69 Newland's mounted man-at-arms** (`newlandrider_p1`): mail and helm, the Sun's orange-gold surcoat and caparison, a round shield, a longsword, on a bay warhorse. 10 pages, 175 drawings. Rows Idle 4, Walk 8, Charge 6, Strike 6, Flinch 4, Fall 6.
- **ART-70 the plains tribes' outrider** (`plainsrider_p1`): long dark hair, brown leather, a spear, a cased bow, on a lean dun plains horse with a feathered mane. 10 pages, 175 drawings, the same rows.
- **ART-71 the covered wagon** (`wagon_p1`): dark iron-bound wood, purple-trimmed sideboards, plain canvas (toned per side by the engine), two draft horses, a wagoneer in black with a purple sash. 5 pages, 114 drawings. Rows Idle 4, Roll 8, Jolt 4, Wreck 6.
The refs are the game's own paladin, barbarian and warlock at 3x (from the ART-SNOOTNPCS kit). When they come back: a `charge` row is new to the engine, a mounted group needs its own sheet in the Snoot's show, and the wagon is new code on the map and in the show (the Snoot lane §19).

### Barley's second shot, ART-77 (asked 10-20 in §16; drawn 10-20 by the art desk, partial; CUT 10-20, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)


```text
BARLEY -- the same man as ART-75's identity page (ATTACH deep16/_src/barley/ART-75/ART-75_IDENTITY_PAGE-1.png): lean, wiry, young, a serious angular clean-shaven face under rough straw-blond hair; a weathered medium-brown open coat with a raised collar and a ragged uneven hem at the knees, torn sleeves to the forearms, a dark open-neck shirt, dark loose trousers, bare feet; one wooden threshing flail, two-handed -- the long staff and the short beater on its hinge. Draw him exactly as that page does, the same size and colours. His SECOND WIND and ACTION SURGE as drawn in ART-75 bent him into a squat: both are redrawn here STANDING -- the knees never bend below a standing stance, no crouch, no lean below the hips. His WALK is drawn again for SE, NE and E: eight frames in two half-strides, the right leg leading 1-4 (1 contact, 2 passing, 3 contact, 4 passing) and the left leg leading 5-8 the same way; the near leg drawn in front where the legs cross; the model that worked is ATTACH deep16/_src/barley/ART-76/pass2/ART-76_16BIT_HEAD-3_PAGE-2-v2.png (his S walk as cut).

HEAD 1 -- BARLEY from the bottom-right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the bottom-right corner of the image. Rows: SecondWind (6: 1 the guard, 2-3 the flail's butt planted and one deep breath that lifts the chest and shoulders, 4 a hand pressed to the ribs, 5-6 the guard back up -- standing throughout); ActionSurge (6: 1 the guard, 2-3 the flail snapped up to a high ready across the chest, 4 the weight onto the front foot and one short step, 5 the eyes on the target, 6 the guard -- standing throughout); Walk (8: as the look says, two half-strides); GetUp (6: 1 flat on the back as the Fall leaves him, 2 a palm to the ground, 3 sat up with the flail across the knees, 4 on one knee, the flail's butt planted, 5 rising on it, 6 the guard -- the same hand keeps the flail from 3 to 6).

HEAD 2 -- BARLEY from the top-right, seen from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the top-right corner of the image, rows only. Rows: SecondWind (6: as HEAD 1, from behind); ActionSurge (6: as HEAD 1, from behind); Walk (8: as the look says, two half-strides, from behind).

HEAD 3 -- BARLEY from the front: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing straight toward the viewer, rows only. Rows: SecondWind (6: as HEAD 1, from the front); ActionSurge (6: as HEAD 1, from the front); Fall (6: struck, 1 reeling back, 2-3 the knees going, 4 down on his side, 5-6 flat on his back, the flail fallen beside his right hand -- the flail on the same side as his right hand in every frame); Prone (2: lying as Fall 6 leaves him, 1 still, 2 a breath); GetUp (6: as HEAD 1, from the front, rising from the Fall's lie).

HEAD 4 -- BARLEY in profile from the right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing the right edge of the image, rows only. Rows: SecondWind (6: as HEAD 1, in profile); ActionSurge (6: as HEAD 1, in profile); Walk (8: as the look says, two half-strides, in profile -- the near leg in front at the crossing).

HEAD 5 -- BARLEY from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing straight away from the viewer, rows only. Rows: SecondWind (6: as HEAD 1, from behind); ActionSurge (6: as HEAD 1, from behind); Walk (8: as the look says, two half-strides, from behind -- the flail carried on his right shoulder, the right hand at the hinge end and the left lower on the staff, the same grip in all eight frames; from behind his right hand is on the screen's right); GetUp (6: as HEAD 1, from behind); Victory (6: 1 the guard, 2-4 the right hand lifted in a fist, the left holding the flail upright, 5-6 the guard -- the right hand is on the screen's right from behind); FlailOverhead (6: 1 the guard, 2-3 the flail raised overhead with both hands, the same hand nearest the hinge in both, 4 the strike down and forward, 5 the follow-through low, 6 the guard).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no portraits, no turnarounds, no title; every sheet draws him exactly as the identity page does, the same look, colours and size. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame faces the way its HEAD says; a walk is animated in place, not travelling across the sheet. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. Feet on one baseline. No scenery.
```
Cut as: tools/barley-sheet.py, the same rows and names into `barley_p2` (secondwind, actionsurge, walk SE NE E, hurt S, prone S, getup SE S N, victory N, attack2 N); each walk wired only if `python tools/barley-sheet.py legs` reads it alternating.

### ART-97 -- Vilar, the Show-Armorer (content/shops.json `vilar`) (shop:vilar)

```text
VILAR, SILVERTON SHOPKEEPER: TALKING PORTRAIT AND TOWN FIGURE (one image a HEAD, seven). ATTACH deep16/_src/winters/ART-78/ART-78_HEAD-1_PAGE-1.png and ART-78_HEAD-1_PAGE-6.png (the shape of the portrait pages: Winters' Idle and Acquisition -- for the format only, not the face) and deep16/_src/art-desk/sent/REF-2026-10-21-town-figure-size.png (the size of the town figure: two of the heroes as the game draws them, 4x).

VILAR -- the show-armorer. Mid-years, ambiguous of gender, built like someone used to swinging weight -- thick through the shoulders and wrists. Dark hair tied back off a soot-marked face. Both hands burn-scarred across the fingers. Heavy scorched apron, a leather thumb-guard [seat]. A patient half-smile, and the flat evaluating gaze of someone reading a blade's balance from across a room. Vilar is written they/them: keep the ambiguity on the page.

HEAD 1 -- IDLE (6, two bands of three): a breathing and blink loop at the counter, hands resting on it; a half-close and a blink in frames 4-5; loops cleanly.
HEAD 2 -- ATTENTIVE (1, one drawing): neutral listening.
HEAD 3 -- WELCOMING (1, one drawing): restrained warmth.
HEAD 4 -- SKEPTICAL (1, one drawing): assessing an offer.
HEAD 5 -- CONCERNED (1, one drawing): a guarded refusal.
HEAD 6 -- WORK (6, two bands of three): lifts a short stage blade across two fingers to find its balance, sights down the edge with one eye, then lays it on the counter (anatomical right hand does the lifting; the left stays on the counter); the last frame the hands back at rest.
HEAD 7 -- STANDING, FACING SOUTH (the town floor's figure): Idle (4: standing at ease in the heavy scorched apron over dark work clothes, the leather thumb-guard, dark hair tied back, burn-scarred hands at the sides, a slow breath; the same pose on every frame but the breath).

HEADs 1-6 (the talking portrait): 16-bit pixel-art cut-scene portrait animation, comparable to the art desk's ART-78 (Ambrose Winters) and Sylvia Swann. Waist-up behind a plain dark wooden counter ledge, a stable eye-level three-quarter front camera, looking screen left at someone just beside the camera; the head and body the same size on every page as on the Idle page. Crisp pixel clusters and restrained shading. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). Each image 1440 x 810, ONE action an image, six-frame actions in two bands of three with clear gutters; the counter ledge under the hands on every frame, its top edge at the same height. A title and frame numbers as on ART-78's pages, kept well clear of the drawings (a gap of navy between the title and the top of the hair). No scenery, no shop interior, no UI or border.

HEAD 7 (the town figure): 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game -- the grid's own figures; every frame facing straight toward the viewer (south). The whole figure head to foot, the same size and proportions as the attached figures (the four heroes: a grown man stands about 50 pixels from sole to crown at game size; draw it large, the cutter brings it down). Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no portrait, no other panels, no title. One labelled row of frames, its name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it. Feet on one baseline. No scenery, no counter.
```
Cut as: the portrait by `tools/portrait-sheet.py` (a `PEOPLE` line: `deep16/_src/vilar/<id>/`, the default layout) into `pt_vilar`, a line in `deep16/js/counter.js` C.VENDORS (its first-game shop on the line, Griz's word); the figure, HEAD 7, its S idle cut into `vilar_p1` (rows `idle`, S only; `tools/sheetrows.py`, measured beside the heroes in its STANDS) and stood at the counter in place of the stand-in (T.goIn).

```text
HEAD 6 -- WORK (6, two bands of three): wraps a small stoppered vial in a twist of brown paper, ties it with thread, and sets it on the counter (the shop's line: "Mama mixes, I sell"); the last frame the hands back at rest.
HEAD 7 -- STANDING, FACING SOUTH (the town floor's figure): Idle (4: standing small and upright in a dark green robe with gold trim, white hair, the bearing of the woman who runs the Hex, a slow breath; the same pose on every frame but the breath).

HEADs 1-6 (the talking portrait): 16-bit pixel-art cut-scene portrait animation, comparable to the art desk's ART-78 (Ambrose Winters) and Sylvia Swann. Waist-up behind a plain dark wooden counter ledge, a stable eye-level three-quarter front camera, looking screen left at someone just beside the camera; the head and body the same size on every page as on the Idle page. Crisp pixel clusters and restrained shading. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). Each image 1440 x 810, ONE action an image, six-frame actions in two bands of three with clear gutters; the counter ledge under the hands on every frame, its top edge at the same height. A title and frame numbers as on ART-78's pages, kept well clear of the drawings (a gap of navy between the title and the top of the hair). No scenery, no shop interior, no UI or border.

HEAD 7 (the town figure): 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game -- the grid's own figures; every frame facing straight toward the viewer (south). The whole figure head to foot, the same size and proportions as the attached figures (the four heroes: a grown man stands about 50 pixels from sole to crown at game size; draw it large, the cutter brings it down). Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no portrait, no other panels, no title. One labelled row of frames, its name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it. Feet on one baseline. No scenery, no counter.
```

### The top-down places' tiles and props: Silverton, the Guano Mine's top levels, the Glowseep (asked 10-22 in art-wanted section 21; drawn 10-21/22 by the art desk, ART-111 to ART-113; CUT 10-22, `da7400c`, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

Moved here whole 2026-10-22 by the top tiles seat, on his *"art 111-113 are in to see if we can give the top down something to work with"*. Every HEAD came back (paginated by the art desk's thirty-drawing ceiling: ART-111 twelve pages, ART-112 six, ART-113 four), with its known defects named in its CUTTER-NOTES. What was laid and what was not, and why, is the return note `deep16/_src/art-desk/sent/ENGINE-RETURN-2026-10-22-top-down-tiles.md`; for a re-roll, its OPEN list.


His words, 2026-10-22 (to the lanes desk): *"have the 2d places, silverton, surface guano mines and gallieries, the wet - compose a tile art request that includes variants and any props they want"*, then *"i meant glowseep, not wet"*. Read as every floor drawn top-down, north up, on the town's engine (`deep16/js/topview.js`): Silverton's streets and the rooms behind its doors (`deep16/js/town.js`); the Guano Mine's top levels -- the Crawler Warrens (`warrens_a` the mouth camp, `warrens_b` the stalls, `warrens_c` the workings) and the Guano Galleries (`galleries_g1` the mouth, `galleries_g2` the working gallery, `galleries_g3` the dens; `deep16/js/mines.js`); and the Glowseep (`M.SEEP_ROWS`). Every tile there is drawn by code today: one 48 px square a tile, four noise variants a kind, no edges or corners, one box for every crate, tent and sledge, one stone ring for every well, beam and fire pit, no flame, no stairs, no trees, no lamps. What he has said of the look: *"Silverton and the warrens prettier versions of what we had in the 8 bit"*; *"The roof brown and the road brown are too close together"*; *"Can we get some diagonal looking tiles to try and make the hex look better?"*; of the working gallery, *"giant brown blur, really"*; of the dens, *"too often hidden behind walls that should probably transluce"* (the see-through wall is the engine's job; the art gives the walls their faces). Section 17's shop props (HELD 10-21 *"till the town's tiles please him"*) are folded in here as the Silverton block's HEAD 7, on these words; section 17 stays as it was written. Sent to the art desk as `deep16/_src/art-desk/sent/ASK-2026-10-22-top-down-tiles.md` (carried 10-22, his *"outbox delivered"*).

```text
SILVERTON, TOP-DOWN (one image a HEAD):

SILVERTON -- a poor frontier mining town at the foot of a dwarven mountain, an early-Renaissance town of honest, worn things: timber-framed houses with pale plaster between dark beams, clay-tile, slate and shingle roofs, dusty streets, a dressed-stone plaza with a fountain, a river one tile wide, mine headframes over shafts at the town's edge, a six-sided timber fighting arena called the Hex. The roofs and the roads must never share a colour: the roads a pale dusty tan, the roofs red clay, blue-grey slate, cool grey shingle and pale stone. One tile is one step of a 16-bit RPG town map: a person stands about one tile tall, a house is two to four tiles across.

HEAD 1 -- THE GROUND: square ground tiles, four variants of each kind in a row, every variant tiling seamlessly with the others of its kind: Beaten earth (bare tan ground, a few pebbles) - Dirt road (paler dusty tan, faint wheel ruts: the streets) - Grass (sparse dry tufts on earth) - Dressed stone (square grey flagstones in staggered courses: the Gate Plaza and Fountain Street) - Threshold (a worn stone step-slab before a door) - Fountain water (clear blue, two frames of glints) - River water (deeper blue-green, moving, two frames) - Spoil (mine tailings: grey rubble scattered on earth). Then the edges, a full set for each (north, south, east, west, the four outer corners, the four inner corners): grass against earth; the dressed stone's kerb against the dirt road; the river's banks against earth. Then THE BANK: an earth bank one tile tall facing south, a stone lip along its top -- its left end, its middle, its right end -- and a stair of rough stone steps down it, two tiles wide.

HEAD 2 -- THE ROOFS: each roof as nine pieces that tile across a house of any size (north-west corner, north edge, north-east corner, west edge, middle, east edge, south-west corner, south edge, south-east corner; the ridge running east-west through the middle, the north slope in shadow, the south slope lit), and the same roof on a one-tile house: Red clay tile (the tall houses) - Blue-grey slate (the low houses and booths) - Cool grey shingle (the shacks: grey, not brown) - Pale stone (the tower) - and a wall-walk of grey blocks (the top of a compound's stone wall).

HEAD 3 -- THE WALLS, DOORS AND SIGNS: the south faces below the roofs, one tile wide each, two heights (a low house's two-thirds of a tile, a tall house's full tile), each with a left end, a middle and a right end: Timber-framed plaster (dark posts every half tile, a beam, pale plaster) - the same with a shuttered window - the same with a lit window (warm yellow) - Grey stone (the tower, the compound) - then the doors, each one tile wide in its wall: a store door standing open (a plank door ajar in a stone frame under a lintel) - a door shut and barred - a wide double door, iron-banded. Then the shop signs: a hanging plank sign on two chains from an iron bracket, blank, and the same sign with each of these painted on it, one each: a ring, a pair of scales, a coin, a potion bottle, a bed, a star, a pack, a candle, a sword, a needle and thread, a jar, a sun, a mortar and pestle.

HEAD 4 -- THE HEX AND THE MOUNTAIN: the Hex is a six-sided timber arena, so its pieces run on the diagonals: a log wall face for each of its six sides (two running east-west, four on the diagonals), the corners where two sides meet, its roof ring of pale stone over the stands in the same six directions, its south-tip double doors, and its floor of raked gold sand (four variants). The mountain at the town's north edge: its rock top (four variants, dark purple-grey stone, cracked), its cliff face one tile wide and a tile and a third tall (left end, middle, right end), and a vault door set into the cliff (iron bars over heavy planks).

HEAD 5 -- THE STREET'S PROPS, each drawn alone and labelled beside it: Market stall (a plank counter under a striped cloth awning, two tiles) - Crate - Barrel - Sacks - Handcart (two-wheeled) - Well (a stone ring with a winch and bucket) - Mine headframe (a timber tower over a shaft mouth, two by two tiles) - Lamp post (an iron post with a hooded lantern, lit) - Pine tree (gnarled, its crown over two by two tiles) - Bench - Hitching rail - Fountain (a round stone basin with a spout, two by two tiles) - Signpost - Notice board (a plank board on two posts, papers pinned to it).

HEAD 6 -- THE ROOMS' FLOORS AND WALLS (inside the shops, seen the same way): Plank floor, Flagstone floor, Earth floor (four variants each); the back wall seen from the south, a tile and a little tall: plaster between dark timbers above a skirting board (left end, middle, right end), the same with a window, the same with a stone fireplace, lit; an inside doorway with its door standing open.

HEAD 7 -- THE ROOMS' PROPS, each drawn alone and labelled beside it: Counter (a shop counter of dark varnished wood, waist high, its top worn pale, two tiles long, its front facing south) - Shelves (wall shelves to a man's head, packed with jars, folded cloth, small boxes and ledgers, their back against a north wall, two tiles long) - Grille (a moneychanger's counter with an iron grille above it, a slot at the bottom and a small brass pair of scales on top, two tiles long) - Beam scale (a weigh-house's great balance: a heavy timber beam on a stout post, two broad pans on chains, iron weights at its foot, two by two tiles) - Anvil (on a stump block) - Forge (a stone hearth with glowing coals, a leather bellows and a smoke hood, two tiles long) - Barrel - Sacks (grain sacks stacked three high) - Cloth rack (bolts of dyed cloth, red, blue and ochre) - Desk (a scrivener's slanted desk, an inkpot, a quill, an open ledger) - Pew (a plain wooden bench with a back, two tiles long) - Lantern rack (small brass lanterns, three lit) - Table (two tiles) - Chair - Barber's chair - Bath (a wooden tub, steaming) - Bed (a wooden frame bed with a blanket) - Stable stall (a plank partition with a manger).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art tiles and props, SNES-era top-down RPG (A Link to the Past's look), crisp dark outline where a thing stands up, readable at small size. Seen from above and a little to the south, north up: the top of a thing from above and its south face below it; light from the upper left. Every tile square and the same size, 48 by 48 pixels, on an even grid with a 1-pixel gap, each labelled beside it, the variants of a kind side by side in a row. Props each alone with clear space around it, all at one scale: a thing on one tile fills it, a thing on two lies across two side by side. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title; nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. No characters, and no labels on the tiles or props themselves.
```
Cut as: tiles by a `tools/toptiles-sheet.py` into `toptiles_p1`; `deep16/js/topview.js` tileCanvas draws HEAD 1's grounds in place of its noise (its four hashed variants take the four), the edges by a square's neighbours (new), the bank and stair on T.DROP's face; standCanvas draws HEAD 2's roofs by `T.ROOFS` (`b`/`u` red clay, `n` slate, `h`/`j` shingle, `B` stone, `q` the wall-walk) and HEAD 3's faces, doors (`T.SHUT` the barred one) and signs (TV.SIGNS: ring Winters, scales Hessle, coin Kessler, potion Calla, bed Cassia, star Lisbet, pack Marko, candle Kasten, sword Vilar, stitch Davos, jar Percy, sun Aldwin, mortar Mama); hexCanvas HEAD 4's Hex; HEAD 5 and HEAD 7 stand on their squares in place of TV.BLOCK's code-drawn boxes (`k` stall and crate, `w` well, `K` counter, `S` shelves, `f` rail) and the room furniture `deep16/js/town.js` lays (T.room).

```text
THE GUANO MINE'S TOP LEVELS, TOP-DOWN (one image a HEAD):

THE GUANO MINE -- a bat-guano mine under a dwarven mountain, worked by poor crews: the Crawler Warrens, where carrion crawlers are penned and milked in timber cradles and their stalls are lit along a cart track; and the Guano Galleries, where the crust of bat guano is scraped off the cave floors, roped down ladders and slid down a chute to the dens below. Outside, at each mouth, a crews' camp at the mountain's foot: sheds, pens, drying racks, a river with a plank bridge. Inside, the colours must not run together into one brown: the cave rock cool grey-violet, the cave floor a darker grey-brown, the guano a cream-and-ochre crust that is plainly the bright thing on the floor, the deep guano wet and dark, the gravel pale grey. One tile is one step of a 16-bit RPG map: a person stands about one tile tall.

HEAD 1 -- THE CAVE GROUND: square ground tiles, four variants of each kind in a row, every variant tiling seamlessly with the others of its kind: Cave floor (cool dark stone, cracks) - Guano (a cream and ochre crust over the cave floor, white flecks) - Deep guano (a wet dark brown heap, sticky underfoot) - Gravel (pale grey grit: the sieving floor) - Bones (cave floor strewn with small bones) - Old webbing (cave floor under grey strands) - Fungus bed (dark soil with violet and bone-white caps) - Rails (a cart track of iron rails on wooden sleepers over cave floor: straight north-south, straight east-west, a bend, an end) - Pit (a black shaft mouth with a broken lip) - Stair down (rough steps going down into the dark). Then the edges, a full set (north, south, east, west, the four outer corners, the four inner corners): guano's crust against the cave floor, and deep guano against guano.

HEAD 2 -- THE ROCK AND THE TIMBER: Cave rock top (four variants, cool grey-violet stone, not brown) - the rock's face where it stands above a floor to its south, one tile wide and a tile and a third tall (left end, middle, right end) - the same face shored with timber (two posts and a cap beam) - a mine door in the face (planks in an iron frame) - a mouth in the face seen from outside (a dark opening under a timber lintel) - a carved rune wall (faint runes in the face) - a chimney (a natural vent in the rock, a dark hole with soot around it) - a ladder up a face, and a ladder laid flat down a slope - the slide head (a smooth chute of polished guano going down into the dark).

HEAD 3 -- THE CAMPS OUTSIDE THE MOUTHS (in the open at the mountain's foot; their earth, dirt road and grass are Silverton's): a river one tile wide (two frames) with its banks (north, south, the ends) and a plank bridge across it (its two ends and its middle) - the pens' stockade (heavy timber posts with a rail: straight east-west, straight north-south, a corner, a gate) - a milking cradle (a low timber frame with leather straps, one tile) - a guano smoke rack (a timber drying rack hung with sacking) - a guano sledge - the weigh beam (a timber beam on a post with a hanging pan) - a crews' bunkhouse's wall face and door (rough planks) - The Board (a notice board on two posts, papers pinned to it).

HEAD 4 -- THE MINE'S PROPS, each drawn alone and labelled beside it: Lantern post (an iron post with a hooded lantern, lit, and the same unlit) - Wall lantern (hung on a rock face, lit and unlit) - Crate - Iron-banded chest - Mine cart (on the rails) - Guano sacks - A pick and a shovel leaning together - A shoring post (a free-standing timber post and cap beam) - A crew's bunk (a timber bunk bed) - A water barrel.

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art tiles and props, SNES-era top-down RPG (A Link to the Past's look), crisp dark outline where a thing stands up, readable at small size. Seen from above and a little to the south, north up: the top of a thing from above and its south face below it; light from the upper left. Every tile square and the same size, 48 by 48 pixels, on an even grid with a 1-pixel gap, each labelled beside it, the variants of a kind side by side in a row. Props each alone with clear space around it, all at one scale: a thing on one tile fills it, a thing on two lies across two side by side. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title; nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. No characters, and no labels on the tiles or props themselves.
```

```text
THE GLOWSEEP, TOP-DOWN (one image a HEAD):

THE GLOWSEEP -- a bog at the foot of a dwarven mountain where glow-moss grows on the mud, draining by a dark channel toward a lake; a road along its edge; a camp of three old light-crew hands by the road, who go out at night with long poles and lanterns to gather the moss. By day it is olive mud, reeds and tussock grass; by night the moss glows a soft blue-green. One tile is one step of a 16-bit RPG map: a person stands about one tile tall.

HEAD 1 -- THE BOG GROUND: square ground tiles, four variants of each kind in a row, every variant tiling seamlessly with the others of its kind: Mud (olive-black, a wet sheen) - Reeds (tall reeds standing in mud) - Tussock grass (dry grass tufts on wet ground) - Glow moss by day (low green moss on mud) - Glow moss by night (the same moss, its spots glowing soft blue-green) - Still pool (black-green water, two frames) - The drain (a dark channel of moving water, two frames) - Stepping stones (a ford across the drain) - A log causeway (split logs laid across the mud: straight east-west, straight north-south, a bend). Then the edges, a full set (north, south, east, west, the four outer corners, the four inner corners): mud against grass, and the pool's and the drain's banks against mud.

HEAD 2 -- THE CAMP AND THE CREW'S THINGS, each drawn alone and labelled beside it: Tent (a canvas ridge tent, its flap open, two tiles) - Campfire (a ring of stones with logs, burning in four frames, and the same fire burned down to embers) - Light pole (a tall pole with a hooded lantern at its top, lit) - Moss pole (a long pole with a hook, leaning) - Offering stump (a tree stump with small offerings on it: coins, a stub of candle, a ribbon) - Bedroll - Moss basket (a wicker basket of glowing moss) - Duckboards (a plank walkway over mud: straight and a corner).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art tiles and props, SNES-era top-down RPG (A Link to the Past's look), crisp dark outline where a thing stands up, readable at small size. Seen from above and a little to the south, north up: the top of a thing from above and its south face below it; light from the upper left. Every tile square and the same size, 48 by 48 pixels, on an even grid with a 1-pixel gap, each labelled beside it, the variants of a kind side by side in a row. Props each alone with clear space around it, all at one scale: a thing on one tile fills it, a thing on two lies across two side by side.
```
