### The townsfolk of Fountain Street (asked 10-08 in the wanted file's §2; drawn by the art desk, ART-09 10-10 and ART-52 10-18; CUT 10-27, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

The Skylights' walk-in (10-05): the street's people running from the trolls (`deep16/js/battle.js` townsfolk), in every direction.

```text
THE TOWNSFOLK -- three plain people of a mountain town for a tactics game, drawn on two sheets: a man in a work shirt, a leather apron and boots; a woman in a long skirt with a shawl over her shoulders; a child of about eight in a smock. Ordinary, unarmed, frightened. On every sheet each is drawn on their own rows, the row's name saying whose it is, the man, the woman and the child at their true heights against each other.

HEAD 1 -- THE TOWNSFOLK from the bottom-right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the bottom-right corner of the image. Rows, for each of the three in turn: Idle (4: standing, glancing about nervously); Run (8: running toward the bottom-right for their life, arms pumping, looking back over a shoulder).

HEAD 2 -- THE TOWNSFOLK from the top-right, seen from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the top-right corner of the image, rows only. Rows, for each of the three in turn: Idle (4: standing, glancing about nervously, their faces hidden); Run (8: running toward the top-right for their life, arms pumping, one glance back over a shoulder).

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no portraits, no turnarounds, no title; every later sheet draws the three exactly as the first sheet does, the same looks, colours and sizes. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame faces the way its HEAD says; a run is animated in place, not travelling across the sheet. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. Feet on one baseline. No scenery.
```

### Aldwin of the mission chapel: a talking portrait and a south-facing town figure (asked 10-22 in art-wanted section 22; drawn 10-27 by the art desk, ART-114; CUT 10-28, `be71779`, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

Aldwin keeps the mission chapel's free floor (`chapel:aldwin`, pin 30): since `d36e913` (the services seat) his scene runs on the counter screen -- his first-game word, then SLEEP, free -- with the counter's plain shape where his face goes. He is not Aldwyn Androit the notary (pin 5, `pt_androit`). He has no number in the art desk's ART-78-SHOPKEEPERS-INDEX; his identity is the art desk's own `silverton-portrait-sheets.md` (its third, *[seat]* marks kept). The block is the keepers' shape (section 20's, now on `deep16-art-in-hand.md`): six portrait pages and the town figure facing south. Copy in the outbox as `deep16/_src/art-desk/sent/ASK-2026-10-22-aldwin.md`, to carry when the ceiling lifts.

```text
ALDWIN, SILVERTON'S MISSION CHAPEL: TALKING PORTRAIT AND TOWN FIGURE (one image a HEAD, seven). ATTACH deep16/_src/winters/ART-78/ART-78_HEAD-1_PAGE-1.png and ART-78_HEAD-1_PAGE-6.png (the shape of the portrait pages: Winters' Idle and Acquisition -- for the format only, not the face) and deep16/_src/art-desk/sent/REF-2026-10-21-town-figure-size.png (the size of the town figure: two of the heroes as the game draws them, 4x).

ALDWIN -- the mission almoner. An aging priest, severe and watchful, in threadbare grey wool with a plain wooden token at the throat [seat]. Hands calloused from kneading bread, not from prayer. Quiet certainty in the face, and behind it the willingness to protect the guilty. Fifty years at the bread line at a frontier mission of the sun god's faith, far past its own writ: bread at six, at quarter past for the proud; the floor free, if the old man likes your face.

HEAD 1 -- IDLE (6, two bands of three): a breathing and blink loop at the counter, hands resting on it; a half-close and a blink in frames 4-5; loops cleanly.
HEAD 2 -- ATTENTIVE (1, one drawing): neutral listening.
HEAD 3 -- WELCOMING (1, one drawing): restrained warmth -- the floor offered, not a smile.
HEAD 4 -- SKEPTICAL (1, one drawing): weighing the face in front of him.
HEAD 5 -- CONCERNED (1, one drawing): a guarded refusal.
HEAD 6 -- WORK (6, two bands of three): takes up a round loaf from the counter, breaks it in two with his calloused hands, holds one half out toward the party (screen left), lays the other half down; the last frame the hands back at rest (anatomical right hand does the holding out; the left stays near the counter).
HEAD 7 -- STANDING, FACING SOUTH (the town floor's figure): Idle (4: standing at ease in the threadbare grey wool, the wooden token at his throat, flour on his sleeves, hands folded before him, a slow breath; the same pose on every frame but the breath).

HEADs 1-6 (the talking portrait): 16-bit pixel-art cut-scene portrait animation, comparable to the art desk's ART-78 (Ambrose Winters) and Sylvia Swann. Waist-up behind a plain dark wooden counter ledge, a stable eye-level three-quarter front camera, looking screen left at someone just beside the camera; the head and body the same size on every page as on the Idle page. Crisp pixel clusters and restrained shading. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). Each image 1440 x 810, ONE action an image, six-frame actions in two bands of three with clear gutters; the counter ledge under the hands on every frame, its top edge at the same height. A title and frame numbers as on ART-78's pages, kept well clear of the drawings (a gap of navy between the title and the top of the hair). No scenery, no chapel interior, no UI or border.

HEAD 7 (the town figure): 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game -- the grid's own figures; every frame facing straight toward the viewer (south). The whole figure head to foot, the same size and proportions as the attached figures (the four heroes: a grown man stands about 50 pixels from sole to crown at game size; draw it large, the cutter brings it down). Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no portrait, no other panels, no title. One labelled row of frames, its name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it. Feet on one baseline. No scenery, no counter.
```

### The Hex's inside: its three floors' tiles and props (asked 10-22 in art-wanted section 23; drawn 10-27 by the art desk, ART-115, best available; CUT 10-28 in part, `1ffd41b`, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

Moved here whole 2026-10-28 by the second cutter seat (started by the lanes desk on his *"spin up a second cutter, more landing in fresh"*). Every HEAD came back, some failing the art desk's own notes; what was laid and what was not is on the line above and in the return. The block as it was asked:


His words: to the lanes desk, 2026-10-22, *"start a Silverton Hex interior job, let it request it's own tile sheet as needed"*; 10-21, *"Inside of the hex will probably be N/S tile map rather than grid"* and *"Can we get some diagonal looking tiles to try and make the hex look better?"*; to the seat's questions, *"1 - build all 3, where 3 can be just the meeting room and the offices upstairs and currently turned off for player access"*. The Hex's inside is built top-down, north up, on the town's engine (`deep16/js/hex.js`, landed 10-22): its three floors at 5 ft a square, laid from the walkable's measured plan (the realm's `the-hex.md`), every piece drawn by code today on its true diagonal. Section 21's Silverton block HEAD 4 asked for the Hex from outside, and it is in hand (ART-111, cut 10-22 into `toptiles_p1`, `da7400c`): its raked sand and pale stone are laid, inside and out; its walls, corners, roof ring and doors came back drawn angled (isometric), not straight down, so the code's Hex still stands (the top tiles seat's question, folded into this ask by the lanes desk 10-22). HEADs 1-4 ask for what the inside adds; HEAD 5 asks for the outside again, drawn straight down. The sand is ART-111's.

```text
THE HEX, INSIDE, TOP-DOWN (one image a HEAD):

THE HEX -- a six-sided timber fighting arena in a poor frontier mining town, seen from inside with the roof and upper floors cut away, as a 16-bit RPG shows a building's inside. A point of the six-sided shape faces due north and another due south, so four of its six walls run on the diagonals and the other two run straight north-south: every wall, rail, tier and edge piece below comes in those directions. On the ground floor a hexagon of raked gold sand sits in the middle, a board lip round it and two tiers of timber steps going up from it to a plank floor that runs out to the log walls, with four long bars, timber posts holding up the roof, and two spiral stairs by the north point. The second floor is the gambling floor: a ring of floor round a big opening over the sand, with a rail along the opening's edge, red rugs, green felt card tables, and cushioned benches for the slave owners on the south side. The third floor is the town council's meeting room under a big window in the north point, with an office and an armory either side of it. Honest worn timber, dark logs, brass and iron, lamplight. One tile is one step of a 16-bit RPG map: a person stands about one tile tall.

HEAD 1 -- THE FLOORS: square floor tiles, four variants of each kind in a row, every variant tiling seamlessly with the others of its kind: Plank floor (worn boards running east-west, the ground floor's concourse) - Upper tier (timber planks, a lighter step) - Lower tier (timber planks, a darker step) - Board lip (heavy boards at the sand's edge) - Dark polished boards (the council's floor) - Red rug (a deep red woven rug with a gold lattice) - Blue rug (the owners' lounge, the same weave in deep blue) - Doormat (a worn rush mat at a door). Then the edges, each a full set in the six directions (two running east-west, four on the diagonals) with the corners where two meet: the sand against the board lip; the lip stepping up to the lower tier; the lower tier stepping up to the upper tier; the upper tier stepping up to the plank floor -- on the north half each step shows its upright face (a lit timber riser), on the south half only its edge in shadow; the red rug's gold border against the plank floor; the blue rug's border.

HEAD 2 -- THE WALLS FROM INSIDE: the log wall seen from inside, on the two north diagonals: its inner face a tile and a third tall below a dark top, heavy logs laid in courses that follow the diagonal (left end, middle, right end); the same wall running straight north-south (its top only, a tile wide); the two south diagonals as a low stub wall (its top only, so the floor shows beyond it); the corners where two walls meet; the north point's double doors from inside (two plank leaves, the great log standing between them); the south point's open doorway from inside (two door posts); a single door in the straight east or west wall; THE BIG WINDOW: tall glass panes in heavy mullions set into the log wall on the two north diagonals, three tiles each side of the point; an inside timber wall on a diagonal (thin, a tile tall) with a plank door in it, and the same with a serving hatch shut behind two wooden shutters.

HEAD 3 -- THE GROUND FLOOR'S THINGS, each drawn alone and labelled beside it: The long bar on a diagonal (dark wood, its top worn pale, red plank front; an end, the middle, an end), and the same bar straight east-west; The bartender's back shelf (bottles, mugs, a keg tap); The Wall of Boxes (twenty-seven small iron strongboxes, nine across and three high, on a timber rack above the bartender's shelf, a few with names scratched off); An interior post (a round timber log post three feet thick, a wall's height); A spiral stair seen from above (wooden wedge steps winding round a central newel post, a rail round its edge, two tiles across); A guard station (a timber booth wall by a door, waist-high counter); The contraband box (a small iron-bound box with a padlock); A trestle table (plain boards, two benches); The odds slate (a large chalkboard on two legs, chalked columns of names and odds).

HEAD 4 -- THE UPPER FLOORS' THINGS, each drawn alone and labelled beside it: A felt card table (round, green baize, a wooden rim, cards and coin stacks on it) and a stool; The rail at the opening's edge (a timber handrail on turned balusters: straight east-west, on each diagonal, and curved); A cushioned bench (a long settle with deep red cushions); An empty fine chair (high-backed, red velvet); The council's long table (dark polished wood, four tiles long) and a council chair; A heavy desk with papers and an inkwell; An iron strongbox; A weapon rack (spears, axes and round shields); An armory chest (iron-banded); A hanging lantern on a wall hook, lit.

HEAD 5 -- THE HEX FROM OUTSIDE, STRAIGHT DOWN (the town map's view, as the town's houses are drawn: from directly above and a little to the south, NOT isometric, NOT angled): the Hex is a six-sided arena with a point due north and a point due south, so its outline has two straight north-south sides (east and west) and four diagonal sides. Its roof is a ring of pale stone open to the sky in the middle: the ring drawn as flat bands following each of the six sides (stone courses running along the side), the hips where two bands meet, the inner edge round the opening. Under the two south diagonal sides, the log walls' south faces show below the roof edge, the logs laid along the slant; the east and west sides show no face, only the roof's edge. At the south point the wall is cut flat for a pair of iron-banded double doors facing straight south, under the roof's point. Pieces: each roof band (north-west, north-east, east, west, south-west, south-east diagonals and straight runs), the six corners where bands meet, the inner opening's edge on each side, the log wall face on each south diagonal (left end, middle, right end), the south doors closed, and the same doors open.

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art tiles and props, SNES-era top-down RPG (A Link to the Past's look), crisp dark outline where a thing stands up, readable at small size. Seen from above and a little to the south, north up: the top of a thing from above and its south face below it; light from the upper left. Every tile square and the same size, 48 by 48 pixels, on an even grid with a 1-pixel gap, each labelled beside it, the variants of a kind side by side in a row. Props each alone with clear space around it, all at one scale: a thing on one tile fills it, a thing on two lies across two side by side. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title; nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. No characters, and no labels on the tiles or props themselves.
```
Cut as: tiles by a `tools/hexin-sheet.py` into `hexin_p1`; `deep16/js/hex.js` paints HEAD 1 under its floors in place of HX.ground / HX.gambling / HX.third's code (the four variants by the square, the edges by each square's place on the tiers' rings -- its face of six and its band), and stands HEAD 2's walls (HX.pieces: the shell's faces by direction, the carved north doors, the Big Window, the inner walls and the shuttered hatches), HEAD 3's and HEAD 4's things in place of its code-drawn blocks (the four bars, the Wall of Boxes, the posts, the spirals, the guard stations and box, the boards, the slate; the felt tables and stools, the rail's runs and bow, the owners' benches, Vair's chair, the council's table and chairs, the desk, strongbox, racks and chest). HEAD 5 stands in topview.js's hexCanvas (the Hex on the town floor) for the code's roof ring, walls and tip doors. The sand is ART-111's (`hex_sand` in `toptiles_p1`, as js/topview.js TV.piecePx reads it for the Hex's outside). The Hex's people keep their stand-in figures (a figure is a five-HEAD block of its own, not tiles).

### The lamia's human form (asked 10-28 in art-wanted section 24; drawn 10-28 by the art desk, ART-120, PARTIAL; CUT 10-28, `d5372a1d`, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

Moved here whole 2026-10-28 by the first cutter seat (started by the lanes desk; his *"check fresh - one cutter at a time should be fine"*). Every HEAD came back; the walks are open by the art desk's own notes. The block as it was asked:

His words, his ear file of 10-28 (0156Z/0215Z), on the gallery's creatures shelf at the lamia: *"needs human form idle, perhaps others - add art request"*. Since `8f815a3` (the first cutter, the bestiary lane's §37, its lean 1) the lamia stands disguised at the start of a fight -- Disguise Self, the robed woman the art desk drew as frame 6 of her Disguise row -- till her first turn or a wound; on the grid that woman is one still frame (`lamia_p1` row `still`), and she reveals by the Disguise row played backwards (`reveal`). This asks her human form its own rows, five views as RULED 10-14: an Idle to stand in, and a Walk and a Cast for when she keeps the disguise while she moves or casts (Disguise Self lasts an hour and casting does not end it; whether she keeps it past her first turn is his word, below). Her Cast is the gesture alone: since 10-28 the code draws her spell's light at her hand (js/looks.js LK.spark, his *"have code draw it when she casts"*), so the art draws none.

### The lamia's human form
ATTACH `deep16/_src/lamia/ART-20/ART-20_HEAD-1_PAGE-1.png` (her portrait and turnaround: the face, the hair, the gold) and her five Disguise pages -- `ART-20_HEAD-1_PAGE-8.png` (from the bottom-right), `ART-20_HEAD-2_PAGE-7.png` (top-right), `ART-20_HEAD-3_PAGE-7.png` (front), `ART-20_HEAD-4_PAGE-7.png` (right side), `ART-20_HEAD-5_PAGE-7.png` (behind): frame 6 of each is the woman, as she must look in that view.
```text
Attached: a foe from the same game, the lamia, and the five sheets of her Disguise -- frame 6 of each is the ordinary woman she disguises herself as, seen from that sheet's side. Draw that woman: the same face, hair and size as frame 6 shows her, in the same pixel style.

THE LAMIA IN HER HUMAN FORM -- a foe in disguise for a tactics game, drawn on five sheets. An ordinary-looking desert woman standing on two legs: long, wavy black hair falling past her shoulders, dark-lined eyes, a fine gold circlet with one teal stone on her brow, small gold earrings; a plain, loose, hooded robe of sand-coloured cloth with long wide sleeves, gathered at the waist by a broad brown sash, falling to her ankles; simple leather sandals. No dagger, no jewelled collar, no lion's body: nothing about her shows what she is. Calm and watchful, a little too graceful.

HEAD 1 -- THE WOMAN from the bottom-right: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the bottom-right corner of the image. Rows: Idle (6: standing at ease, one hand resting at the front of her robe, a slow breath, the robe's hem and her hair stirring a little; frame 6 matches frame 1); Walk (8: an unhurried, graceful walk toward the bottom-right, the robe swinging; animate in place, not travelling across the sheet); Cast (6: she raises one hand and spreads her fingers toward the bottom-right as if weaving something in the air, holds it, then lowers it; draw NO light, glow, sparkle or magic of any kind -- the game draws the spell itself).

HEAD 2 -- THE WOMAN from the top-right, seen from behind: isometric three-quarter view from above, every frame facing toward the top-right corner of the image, rows only. The same rows as HEAD 1, each drawn from behind facing the top-right: Idle (6); Walk (8); Cast (6, no light).

HEAD 3 -- THE WOMAN straight from the front, facing the viewer: rows only, the same rows and counts as HEAD 1.

HEAD 4 -- THE WOMAN from the right side in profile, facing right: rows only, the same rows and counts as HEAD 1.

HEAD 5 -- THE WOMAN straight from behind, facing away from the viewer: rows only, the same rows and counts as HEAD 1.

Draw each HEAD above as its own separate image, in the order given: one sheet an image, never two sheets in one image. Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art animation sheet, SNES-era action RPG style, crisp dark outline, readable at small size. Isometric three-quarter view from above, the camera looking down at about thirty degrees, as in a tactics game. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). ONE sheet filling the whole image: no other panels, no title. Only the first sheet has a portrait and a turnaround -- top left one large portrait, beside it four stills labelled Front, Right, Back, Left; every later sheet is rows only and draws her exactly as the first sheet's turnaround shows her, the same look, colours and size. Labelled rows of frames, each row's name at its left, numbered 1 to N under each frame -- every number used once, none skipped, one drawing for each number. Every frame faces the way its HEAD says; a walk is animated in place, not travelling across the sheet. Every frame stands apart with clear space around it: nothing overlaps a neighbour or a label. The same scale in every row and on every sheet, feet on one baseline. No scenery except what a row names.
```
Cut as: rows onto `lamia_p1` (tools/lamia-sheet.py) -- Idle -> `still` (the woman she stands as, a breath now where it is one frame), Walk -> `stillwalk`, Cast -> `stillcast` (her spark drawn by the code, tools/castspark.py); HEAD 1 SE, HEAD 2 NE, HEAD 3 S, HEAD 4 E, HEAD 5 N, the cutter mirroring SE for SW, E for W, NE for NW. Each view scaled by the Disguise row's frame 6 of the same view (the woman already on the grid), so she is the same size she reveals from. **Walk and Cast play only on his word:** today she drops the disguise on her first turn (the first cutter's lean 1); kept while she walks and casts and dropped at her first blow or a wound, they would play.

### The generic man and the generic woman (asked 10-28 in art-wanted section 25; drawn 10-28 by the art desk, ART-122; CUT 10-28, `a22dd2e4`, BUILT FROM THE LIST above)

Moved here whole 2026-10-28 by the fourth cutter seat (started by the lanes desk; his *"generic NPCs a  have landed in fresh"*). Every page came back, the woman's last two in a supplement; the art desk's own notes leave the likeness and the fine props for his look. The block as it was asked:


His words, his ear files of 10-28 (relayed by the lanes desk): *"art request for a generic NPC face portrait with 'drinker, gambler, and any other variants we need (guano scraper, etc.) where we do variants instead of 'skeptical' and similar we have for the good NPCs ..."*; and on the first draft's eighteen strangers (relayed ~04:10Z): *"Yes and I want to lean into it. Like "Generic NPC (maybe male and female)" as a specific person with each function as a costume/look they have"*. So two people, each one identity -- the same face, hair and build on every page -- dressed for each job the crowds need: the costumes where the named have their moods (ATTENTIVE, WELCOMING, SKEPTICAL, CONCERNED). They are the man and the woman of the art desk's townsfolk (ART-09 SE and NE, ART-52 S, E and N; cut 10-27 as `folkman_p1` and `folkwoman_p1`, the commoners who flee the Skylights) [seat: the faces then belong to bodies the game already draws]. Today the unnamed of Silverton's street, the Hex, the Crawler Warrens, the Guano Galleries and the Glowseep (content/npcs.json, content/maps/*.json `npcs`, deep16/js/mines.js) speak on a plain card and stand on the floors as the foes' crewman, brawler, bandit and guard (deep16/js/town.js T.KIND); with these, on the counter screen as the named do (deep16/js/counter.js), each in the costume of the job they stand at. The street's boy and girl (`towns4`, `towns8`) are not in it and keep their card [seat].

### The generic man
ATTACH `deep16/_src/townsfolk/ART-52/ART-52_HEAD-3_PAGE-1.png` (the townsfolk from the front: its Man rows are the man) and `deep16/_src/townsfolk/ART-09_HEAD-1_PAGE-1.png` (the same two from the bottom-right), and `deep16/_src/winters/ART-78/ART-78_HEAD-1_PAGE-1.png` and `ART-78_HEAD-1_PAGE-6.png` (the portrait pages' shape, for the format only, not the face).
```text
THE GENERIC MAN -- one ordinary man of a frontier mining town, and the same man on every page: the man of the attached townsfolk sheets -- shaggy brown hair, a full brown beard, a weathered, good-natured face, about forty [seat], a working man's build. On HEAD 1 in the man's own clothes as the sheets show them: a cream shirt with the sleeves rolled, a brown leather apron, blue trousers, brown boots. On every later HEAD dressed for a different job: a costume over the same face, hair and build, the hair and beard combed or tied back as a job wants, never changed; only the clothes and what is in the hands change. Nobody heroic, nobody grotesque.

HEAD 1 -- IDLE (6, two bands of three): in the man's own clothes, a breathing and blink loop at the counter, hands resting on it; loops cleanly.
HEAD 2 -- THE DRINKER (1): a stained work shirt open at the collar, sallow and stubbled, a clay mug of ale held on the ledge, eyes a little too bright.
HEAD 3 -- THE GAMBLER (1): a good coat gone shiny at the cuffs over a waistcoat, rings on two fingers, a fan of cards and a short stack of coins on the ledge, a measuring look.
HEAD 4 -- THE BARKEEP (1): sleeves rolled, a leather barman's apron, a rag over one shoulder, wiping a mug.
HEAD 5 -- THE HIRED HAND (1): a day laborer of the hiring corner: a sweat-stained shirt, a coil of rope over one shoulder.
HEAD 6 -- THE SHAFT HAND (1): a miner off shift: a dusty leather jerkin, a candle-stub helmet pushed back, grime in the creases of the face and knuckles.
HEAD 7 -- THE GUANO SCRAPER (1): a scraper of the Guano Galleries: a cloth tied over nose and mouth pulled down to speak, a hooded lamp, a long-handled scraper, everything powdered pale grey-brown with guano dust.
HEAD 8 -- THE CREW BOSS (1): a tally stick and a hooded lamp, a whistle on a cord, the look of someone counting heads.
HEAD 9 -- THE DRIVE SPEAR (1): a bat-drive hand of the Galleries: a short spear held upright, a leather cap and shoulder guard, watching the dark above.
HEAD 10 -- THE CRAWLER HAND (1): a hand of the Crawler Warrens' pens: leather gloves to the elbow, a milking thimble on a thong at the throat, wary and patient.
HEAD 11 -- THE GUARD (1): a hired guard: a padded jack and an iron cap, a spear haft at the shoulder, bored and watchful.
HEAD 12 -- THE CLERK (1): a warehouse clerk: sleeve-guards and a plain waistcoat, a pen behind the ear, a wax tablet on the ledge.
HEAD 13 -- THE FACTOR'S AGENT (1): a dark, well-cut coat and a high collar, gloved, a sealed letter on the ledge, polite and unreadable.
HEAD 14 -- THE LIGHT-CREW HAND (1): a veteran of the Glowseep's light crews: a long pole over the shoulder, a damp oilcloth cape, a faint green glow of moss on the gloves.

Every HEAD: 16-bit pixel-art cut-scene portrait animation, comparable to the art desk's ART-78 (Ambrose Winters) and Sylvia Swann. Waist-up behind a plain dark wooden counter ledge, a stable eye-level three-quarter front camera, looking screen left at someone just beside the camera; the head and body the same size on every page as on the Idle page. Crisp pixel clusters and restrained shading. Flat dark navy background (a deep blue, not grey and not black). Each image 1440 x 810, ONE action an image, six-frame actions in two bands of three with clear gutters; the counter ledge under the hands on every frame, its top edge at the same height. A title and frame numbers as on ART-78's pages, kept well clear of the drawings. No scenery, no UI or border. HEADs 2 to 14 are one still drawing each: the man of HEAD 1, the face unchanged, in that HEAD's costume, looking screen left as if just spoken to.
```

Where each costume stands in the game (the man or the woman by the seat's lean, except where an id's own words say): own clothes (HEAD 1) -- the street's townsman `towns1` (the man), its townswoman `towns2` (the woman); the drinker -- the Hex's `hexpatron1` (the man), `hexpatron2` (the woman); the gambler -- `gambler1` (the man), `gambler2` (the woman); the barkeep -- the Hex's bars, `bartenderS` (the man), `bartenderE` (the woman); the hired hand -- the street's `towns3` (the man), the Galleries' rope-war `ropewar` (the woman); the shaft hand -- the street's `towns5` and the Warrens' `crew1` (the man), `crew2` (the woman); the guano scraper -- `scraper1` (the man), `scraper2` (the woman); the crew boss -- the scrape boss `scrapeboss` (the woman); the drive spear -- `drivecrew` (the man); the crawler hand -- the Warrens' camp and pens (no id of its own yet); the guard -- the west gate's `guard1` (the man), Dunmore's line `dunmoreLine` (the woman); the clerk -- the warehouse clerk `towns6` (the woman); the factor's agent -- the factor's man `towns7` (the man: his name says so); the light-crew hand -- the Glowseep's eldest `seepcrew1` (the woman), the pole-hand `seepcrew2` (the man: his words say "he"), the quiet one `seepcrew3` (the man).
Cut as: two sheets, `pt_genman` and `pt_genwoman`, by `tools/portrait-sheet.py`: HEAD 1 the idle, each costume a still under its name where the named have their moods; js/counter.js gives each unnamed id its person and costume (the ids above). Town figures in costume are not asked: the man and the woman walk as ART-09 and ART-52 draw them (a body for each costume is thirteen costumes in five views a person, and waits for his word).

### The fountains of Fountain Street
ATTACH `deep16/_src/toptiles/ART-111/ART-111_HEAD-1_PAGE-1.png` (the town's ground tiles: their size, palette and light) and `deep16/_src/toptiles/ART-111/ART-111_HEAD-5_PAGE-1.png` (its fountain, the look of the stone; not its slanted view).
```text
Attached: the top-down tiles of a town in the same game. Draw new pieces for the same town in the same style: the fountains at the foot of a great dwarven waterfall facade -- old dwarven work in pale dressed stone, round basins fed from the falls above.

HEAD 1 -- THE FOUNTAIN, SMALL, seen from directly above (north up, the light from the upper left), 2 x 2 tiles (96 x 96 pixels at game size): a round basin with a thick dressed-stone rim, a low carved pedestal in the middle with a small spout, the water inside still and deep blue-green with a few soft rings round the spout; the rim's south edge shows a little of its outer face. Two frames: the rings moved on.

HEAD 2 -- THE FOUNTAIN, LARGE, the same fountain seen from directly above, 6 x 6 tiles (288 x 288 pixels at game size): the same basin, rim, pedestal and spout, bigger; the water calm, its rings round the spout, the light on it soft -- no bright glare, no foam, no white caustic web. Two frames.

HEAD 3 -- BASIN WATER TILES, seen from directly above, one tile each (48 x 48 at game size): calm deep fountain water in four seamless variants, the same blue-green as HEADs 1 and 2, darker at the edge than the middle; and the water's edge against the stone rim -- straight edges (north, south, east, west), outer corners and inner corners -- so a basin of any size can be built.

Every sheet: 16-bit pixel art, top-down tiles, SNES-era, crisp; the same palette and light as the attached tiles. Flat dark navy background between pieces (a deep blue, not grey and not black), each piece apart with clear space and a label under it. No characters, no scenery beyond what each HEAD names.
```
